<div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-19"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="19" data-folio="xv" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n18_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page xv">[p. xv]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><strong>Papers</strong></h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">READ BEFORE THE BOMBAY BRANCH OF THE EAST INDIA ASSOCIATION OF LONDON <span class="sc">IN</span> 1876.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-21"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="21" data-folio="1" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n20_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 1">[p. 1]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">POVERTY OF INDIA.</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">While</span> pointing out in these notes one of the unfavourable results of the present system of British administration, I do not for a moment mean to ignore the very bright side of British rule, and the many blessings of law and order which it has conferred on India. On the latter subject I have already expressed my sentiments on several occasions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">My object at present is to show in greater detail what I have already stated before, that, under the present system of administration, India is suffering seriously in several ways, and is sinking in poverty. In my humble opinion, this is the question, or rather the most serious question, of the day. Whether I am right or wrong will be for you to judge, after hearing what I have to say. If I am right, I shall have discharged a duty as a loyal subject to urge upon our rulers to remedy this most serious evil. If, on the other hand, I am shown to be wrong, none will rejoice more than myself; and I shall have equally done a duty, as a wrong feeling of a serious character will be removed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">These notes were written two to three years ago.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-001" href="#sec-04-fn-001" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-001" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> I lay them before you as they are. If necessary, I shall consider hereafter any modification that the light of subsequent events may suggest, either in confirmation or refutation of the views expressed in them. There will be a few repetitions from my former papers, but they are necessary in order to make these notes complete. I have endeavoured to avail myself as much as possible of the weight of official or other great authorities, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-22" data-page="22" data-folio="2" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n21_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 2">[p. 2]</button> and facts from official records; hence I shall have more quotations than might be thought suitable in an address before an audience; and my notes may prove dull, but I only hope they may be found of some importance to atone for such dullness. I may propose here that any discussion upon the notes may be deferred till they are all read, and my whole argument placed before you, or otherwise there will be confusion in the discussions.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Total Production of India.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In July, 1870, I made a rough estimate, in my paper on “The Wants and Means of India,” placed before the East India Association, as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The whole produce of India is from its land. The gross land-tax is put down for 1870-71 a little above £21,000,000. Now, I suppose I shall be within the mark if I say that Government takes for this land-tax, on an average, one-eighth of the gross produce, if not more. This gives for the gross production of the country, say, about £168,000,000; add to this—gross opium revenue about £7,000,000; gross salt revenue, £6,000,000; gross forest, £600,000. The total, thus, of the raw produce of the country amounts to under £182,000,000—to be on the safe side, let us say £200,000,000, to include the produce of half a million tons of coal, of alienation lands, or anything else there may be. Now, the population of the whole of British India is nearly 150,000,000; giving, therefore, less than 27s. a head for the annual support of the whole people.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I then further raised the production from £200,000,000 to £300,000,000, to include the value of manufacturing industries, excise on spirits, and a large margin for any omissions, making 40s. a head for the gross production of India as a high estimate.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Since then I have endeavoured to work out the same problem directly, as far as the official data I could get enabled me to do so.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Calcutta Statistical Committee.—Agricultural Tables.</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Parliament requires a yearly report of the moral and material progress of India; and a Statistical Committee is formed at Calcutta to supply the necessary information. This Committee has prescribed certain tables to be filled <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-23" data-page="23" data-folio="3" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n22_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 3">[p. 3]</button> up by the different Governments in their administration reports.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Central Provinces and Burmah reports are the only two complete in their agricultural tables as far as practicable. Four others (Madras, North-West Provinces, Punjab, and Oudh) give them imperfectly. Bengal and Bombay gave the least, or none, up to 1869-70. For what I could not get from the reports I applied to the India Office, which naturally replied they could not give what they did not get from India. It will be seen, therefore, that I have been obliged to work out the production under much difficulty. Not only is the quantity of information insufficient, but the quality even of such as is given is defective. For instance, in the tables of prices of produce in the different districts of the Central Provinces, in order to get an average the prices are added up together, and the total is divided by the number of the districts. This principle is generally adopted by the returns made by all the Governments with respect to average of produce or prices. The principle, however, is altogether fallacious. In taking the average of prices, the quantities of produce sold at the different prices are altogether lost sight of. In the same way, in taking the average produce per acre, the extent of land yielding different quantities is overlooked.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">FALLACY OF ITS STATISTICS.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The result, therefore, is wrong, and all arguments and conclusions based upon such averages are worthless. Taking the instance of the Central Provinces in the administration report of 1867-8, the average price of rice is made out to be Rs. 2-12-7 per maund, when in reality the correct average will be only Rs. 1-8 per maund. Again, the table for the produce of rice per acre gives the average as 579 lbs., when in reality it is 759 lbs. Now, what can be the worth of conclusions drawn from these wrong averages? These averages are not only worthless, but mischievous. It is a pity that, with large Government establishments, more accurate and complete information should not be given. I sincerely trust that future reports will not only work averages upon correct principles, but also work out the total production of their respective provinces. <em>Then</em> only we shall know the actual condition of the mass of the people. All “I thinks” and “my opinions” are of no use on important subjects. The whole <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-24" data-page="24" data-folio="4" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n23_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 4">[p. 4]</button> foundation of all administration, financial and general, and of the actual condition of people, rests upon this one fact — the produce of the country, the ultimate result of all capital, labour and land. With imperfect materials at command, and not possessed of the means to employ a staff to work out all the details as they ought to be, I can only give approximate results.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">How Statistics should be Compiled.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the question of taking proper averages and supplying complete information, I addressed a letter, in February, 1871, to the India Office, which I have reason to believe has been forwarded to the Governments in India. I hope that some attention will be paid to the matter. As a specimen of the correct principle of averages, I have worked out table A of the averages of price and produce of some of the principal productions of the Central Provinces. From this will be seen that the correct average price for rice is Rs. 1-8, instead of Rs. 2-12-7, as stated above; also that the correct average of produce is 759, and not 579 lbs. of rice per acre. I have explained, in the following calculations for the different provinces, the mode I have adopted for each. Though working with insufficient and defective materials, and without the means and time to work out details, I have endeavoured to calculate <em>above</em> the mark, so that, whatever my error, it will be found on the safe side, of estimating a higher produce than the reality.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The principle of my calculations is briefly this. I have taken the largest one or two kinds of produce of a province to represent all its produce, as it would be too much labour for me to work out every produce, great and small. I have taken the whole cultivated area of each district, the produce per acre, and the price of the produce; and simple multiplication and addition will give you both the quantity and value of the total produce. From it, also, you can get the correct average of produce per acre and of prices for the whole province, as in this way you have all the necessary elements taken into account.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Central Provinces.</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The total area of cultivated land (Table 2 of Fiscal Report, 1867-8—an average <em>good season</em> year) is 12,378,215 acres. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-25" data-page="25" data-folio="5" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n24_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 5">[p. 5]</button> price of produce per acre, as worked out in Table A for the important articles rice, wheat, other food-grains, oil-seeds, and cotton is Rs. 11-13-5—say Rs. 12.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-002" href="#sec-04-fn-002" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-002" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> The total value of agricultural produce will be acres 12,378,215 × Rs. 12 = Rs. 14,85,38,580. To this is to be added the produce of Sumbulpore; but the acreage of that district is not given. Making some allowance for it, I increased the produce to, say, Rs. 16,00,00,000, or £16,000,000, for a population of 9,000,000.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have lately met with an unexpected confirmation of my views. The <em>Times of India</em> Summary of 6th June, 1873, takes from the <em>Englishman</em> some particulars from Mr. Pedder’s reply to the Viceroy’s circular on local funds. Mr. Pedder makes out, as the value of produce in the Nagpore district, about Rs. 8 per acre, and my estimate of the whole of the Central Provinces is Rs. 12 per acre. I do not know whether Mr. Pedder has avoided the wrong principles of averages — whether he calculates for an average good season, and whether any allowance is made for bad seasons.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Punjab.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The administration report of 1867-8 gives all the necessary agricultural tables, except one, <em>viz.</em>, the produce per acre of the different kinds of crops. I take this year (1867-8) as a better season, and with a larger extent of cultivation than that of 1868-9.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The chief crops are wheat and other inferior grains—the former nearly 20, and the latter 50 per cent., of the whole cultivation. The price of wheat is higher than that of other inferior grains; and as I take the prices of first-class wheat, I think the average price of the produce of one acre of wheat, applied to the whole cultivated acreage, will be very much above the actual value of the production, and my estimate will be much higher than it ought to be.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-26"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="26" data-folio="6" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n25_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 6">[p. 6]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As the administration reports of both 1867-8 and 1868-9 do not give the produce of crops per acre, I ascertain it from other sources.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the administration report of the Punjab for the year 1850-51 (published in 1854 by the Court of Directors), drawn up by Mr. (now Sir Richard) Temple, a detailed table, dated Jullundhur, 25th October, 1851, gives the produce per acre. The table gives fourteen instances of first-class lands, which, by the rough process of adding up and dividing by the number of instances, gives 14⅐ maunds = 1,160 lbs. (a maund equals 82 lbs.—Report 1855-6); for the <em>second class</em> from eight instances, I find the average 13½ maunds, or 1,107 lbs.; and for the third class from six instances, I find 11 maunds, or 902 lbs. From this table I have taken all at 10 maunds or upwards as representing irrigated land, and the second class, representing the bulk of it, as producing 1,100 lbs. per acre. For unirrigated land I have not sufficient data. I adopt 600 lbs. per acre, for reasons I have stated under heading “North-West Provinces.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After I had made my following calculations on the above basis, I was favoured with a loan from the Record Department of the India Office of the administration report for 1869-70. The produce per acre is given in this report, but the average is taken on the objectionable principle of adding up the produce of all districts and dividing by the number of districts, without reference to the extent of cultivation in each district. According to this, the average of the produce of wheat per acre of all the districts is given in the report as only 624 lbs. The highest produce in three districts included in this average is 1,044, 1,066, and 1,000 lbs.; so that my assumption of 1,100 lbs. per acre for <em>all</em> irrigated land is much above the mark. Again, even making allowance for the drought of the years 1868-9 and 1869-70, my assumption, of 600 lbs. of wheat per acre of all unirrigated land only, is also above the mark.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take the calculated area of 1867-8, which is also the largest of the three years 1867-8, 1868-9, 1869-70; and I take prices for 1867-8, that having been an average good season. The prices of 1868-9 and 1869-70 are scarcity-prices. The year 1867-8 is a fair test for the produce of the Punjab in an average favourable season.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The report for 1867-8 does not give prices of produce for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-27" data-page="27" data-folio="7" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n26_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 7">[p. 7]</button> all districts separately, but only of a few important towns, <em>viz.</em>, Delhi, Umballa, Lahore, Sealkote, Mooltan, and Peshawur (page ciii.); and as I take these prices to represent not only those of the whole of the districts of these towns, but of all the districts of the Punjab, I evidently assume a much higher price than actually must have been the case. My results, therefore, will be affected in a double way (<em>viz.</em>, firstly, in taking first-class wheat to represent all produce; and secondly, in taking the prices in the principal towns to represent all Punjab); and will show then the total value of the production of all Punjab much higher than the reality. I therefore think I shall not be unfair in deducting 10 per cent. as some correction of this double error; and even then I shall be above the mark. The prices given in the report for 1867-8 are as follows (III E. J. Statement, showing the prices of produce in the Punjab for the year 1867-8):—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-001"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-001 · FOLIO 27</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-001_p0027.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a then the average of the six districts to represent the whole of the Punjab in the following calculation (wheat first sort is taken to represent all produce):--</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" 8]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The average value of produce per acre of the irrigated land of the six districts will, therefore, be Rs. 28-7-9.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I now apply this to all irrigated land of the Punjab.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Total irrigated acres are 6,147,038, which, at Rs. 28-7-9 per acre, will give Rs. 17,69,73,224 as the total value of the produce of irrigated land of the Punjab for 1867-8.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I now calculate the value of the produce of unirrigated land (wheat first sort is taken to represent all produce):—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-003"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-003 · FOLIO 28</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-003_p0028.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a class="row-data"><td>Peshawur.</td><td class="num">456,661</td><td>,,</td><td class="num">273,996,600</td><td class="num">41</td><td class="num">66,82,843</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total.</td><td class="num">2,723,058</td><td>....</td><td>....</td><td>..</td><td class="num">3,95,80,882</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The average value of produce of one acre of unirrigated land of the six districts is Rs. 14-5-3. Applying this to the unirrigated land of the whole of the Punjab, the result will be as follows:— Total unirrigated acres 14,810,697, at Rs. 14-5-3 per acre, will give Rs. 21,51,99,427 as the value of the produce of all unirrigated land of the Punjab for 1867-8.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Adding up the value of the produce of irrigated and unirrigated land, the total will be Rs. 39,21,72,651. From this I deduct 10 per cent. for reasons stated above, which will leave Rs. 35,29,54,800 for a population of 17,593,946, or say £36,000,000 for a population of 17,500,000.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">North-West Provinces.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take the figures of 1867-8, being an average good season. The subsequent ones, 1868-9 and 1869-70, have been bad.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The administration report does not give the distribution of chief crops, but I find in the Statistical Reporter of the <em>Indian Economist</em> (page 136) of 15th March, 1871, a table of the crops for 1868-9. From this it will be seen that, out of a total of about 22,000,000 acres, rice, jowari, bajri, wheat, and barley make up—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-004"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-004 · FOLIO 28</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-004_p0028.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-004_p0028.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice</td><td class="num">2,479,874</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Jowari and Bajri</td><td class="num">4,302,890</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Wheat and Barley</td><td class="num">7,257,873</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Acres</td><td>14,040,637 or nearly ⅔</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-29"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="29" data-folio="9" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n28_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 9">[p. 9]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As I cannot get the prices of all the above kinds of produce, except wheat and barley, if I take wheat to represent all, I shall be above the mark.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the administration report of 1868-9 there is a table given of prices of wheat and barley. I take the prices for the months of April, May, and June as those of the good season of 1867-8. The subsequent prices are affected by drought. I should have preferred to take the prices for January to June, 1868; but the table does not give the earlier months. These prices are of some of the chief markets only, so that, taking the prices to represent the whole of the respective districts, and then taking the average of these few districts to represent the whole of the North-West Provinces, the result will be much higher; so, as in the case of the Punjab, I deduct 10 per cent. as some correction for these errors of excess.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The prices given in the report of 1868-9, pages 29, 30, are as follows:—“The following table gives the prices at the close of each month for the year in the chief markets of the provinces. The figures denote seers and chittacks.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-005"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-005 · FOLIO 29</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-005_p0029.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-005_p0029.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" class="num">0</td><td class="num">24</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">23</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">46</td><td class="num">10</td><td>The report also does not say whether these quantities were got for one rupee, but it evidently appears to be meant so.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cawnpore</td><td class="num">23</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">23</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">22</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">22</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">45</td><td class="num">6</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Allahabad</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="row-data"><td>Benares</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="num">15⅓</td><td class="num">35</td><td class="num">14”</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-30"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="30" data-folio="10" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n29_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 10">[p. 10]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The administration reports give no table of produce per acre of different crops. I adopt the same scale as given in the case of the Punjab, for the following additional reasons:— <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-003" href="#sec-04-fn-003" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-003" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>Captain Harvey Tuket’s estimate in the year 1840, from 2,000 experiments, of which 512 were for wheat, made by the Government of the North-West Provinces, gives the average produce of wheat per acre at 1,046 lbs. The late Mr. Thornton, formerly Secretary to that Government, has recorded that, judging from his own experience, he should say that 1,200 lbs. per acre was a high average for irrigated land, and 700 lbs. for that of which a considerable portion is dry.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-004" href="#sec-04-fn-004" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-004" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Mr. Maconochi, in his recent settlements of Oonah (Oudh), gives for irrigated land—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>1st class 21 bushels = 1,218 lbs. (at 58 lbs. per bushel.) 2nd ,, 16 ,, = 928 ,, 3rd ,, 9 ,, = 522 ,,</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">and for unirrigated land—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>1st class 11 bushels = 638 lbs. 2nd ,, 9 ,, = 522 ,, 3rd ,, 7 ,, = 406 ,,</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Taking second class as representing the bulk, the average for irrigated land may be considered as 928 lbs., and for unirrigated 522 lbs. From all the above particulars it will be seen that the estimate I have adopted, of 1,100 lbs. per acre for irrigated and 600 lbs. for unirrigated land, is something above a fair average. A Settlement Officer of the North-West Provinces, in a letter to the <em>Indian Economist</em> of 15th February, 1871 (“Agricultural Gazette,” page 171) sums up all that is known to him on the subject of the produce of wheat per acre in those Provinces. It will be too long an extract to insert here; but, making allowance for the “mischievous fallacy” of all official documents alluded to by this writer, about which I have already complained to the India Office, and which vitiates averages for a number of years or places, I think the average I have adopted above is something more than a reasonable one. When administration reports will give, as they ought, correct particulars for each <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-31" data-page="31" data-folio="11" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n30_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 11">[p. 11]</button> district every year, accurate estimates of the actual produce of the Provinces could be easily made. I give the calculations below. The table of cultivated land, given at page 45 of the appendix to the administration report of 1867-8, does not give the irrigated and unirrigated extent of land separately for the Moradabad, Tarrae, Mynpoorie, Banda and Ghazipore districts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I find that the totals of irrigated and unirrigated land bear nearly the proportion of two-fifths and three-fifths respectively of the whole total cultivated land. I assign the same proportion to the above districts in the absence of actual particulars.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-006"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-006 · FOLIO 31</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-006_p0031.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-006_p0031.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" class="num">3,836,518</td><td>...</td><td>......</td><td>...</td><td class="num">9,31,50,779</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The average value of the produce of one acre will be Rs. 24-2-8.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Applying the average of the above districts to the whole of the irrigated area of the North-West Provinces, the result will be—acres 10,045,050 × Rs. 24-2-8 = Rs. 24,38,93,814.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In a similar manner, the total value of the produce of unirrigated land, as represented by wheat, will be as follows:—</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-32"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="32" data-folio="12" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n31_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 12">[p. 12]</button></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-007"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-007 · FOLIO 32</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-007_p0032.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-007_p0032.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" class="num">372,829,200</td><td>47 6</td><td class="num">75,50,960</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Meerut</td><td class="num">453,694</td><td>„</td><td class="num">272,216,400</td><td>53 10</td><td class="num">406,153</td><td>„</td><td class="num">243,691,800</td><td>48 0</td><td class="num">50,76,912</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Agra</td><td class="num">374,976</td><td>„</td><td 6</td><td class="num">57,73,696</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Allahabad</td><td class="num">644,594</td><td>„</td><td class="num">386,756,400</td><td>35 8</td><td class="row-data"><td>Benares.</td><td class="num">202,818</td><td>„</td><td class="num">121,690,800</td><td>35 14</td><td class="num">33,92,064</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total</td><td class="num">5,007,352</td><td>...</td><td>...</td><td>...</td><td class="num">6,75,58,080</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The average value of wheat per acre of unirrigated land is, therefore, Rs. 13-4-9.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Applying this average to the whole unirrigated land of the North-West Provinces, we get — acres 14,132,111 × Rs. 13-4-9 = Rs. 19,06,42,177. The grand total of the value of the produce of irrigated and unirrigated land will be—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-008"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-008 · FOLIO 32</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-008_p0032.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-008_p0032.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Irrigated</td><td class="num">10,045,050 acres</td><td class="num">Rs. 24,38,93,814</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Unirrigated</td><td class="num">14,132,111 „</td><td>„ 19,06,42,177</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total</td><td class="num">24,177,161 „</td><td>„ 43,45,35,991</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Deducting 10 per cent. for reasons stated above, the remainder will be Rs. 39,10,82,392 for a population of 30,086,898, or say £40,000,000 for a population of 30,000,000.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Bengal.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The administration reports till 1869-70 give no information required by the Statistical Committee, except the area of districts in square miles and acres (report 1869-70). For information for cultivated area, distribution, produce of crops, and prices, I have to look out elsewhere, or make a rough estimate.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">First with regard to the extent of cultivated land, I adopt the following plan as the best I can. The total area of the North-West Provinces is about 50,000,000 acres, of which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-33" data-page="33" data-folio="13" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n32_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 13">[p. 13]</button> about 25,000,000 are cultivated.  The population of those Provinces is, by the late census of 1865, about 30,000,000, so we have the total area 5 acres to 3 persons, and of cultivated area five-sixths of an acre per head.  Now, assuming Bengal to be at least as thickly populated as the North-West Provinces, and the total area, as given in the administration report of 1869 - 70 (appendix, page xxi), being about 105,000,000 acres, the population of Bengal will be about 63,000,000; and I am encouraged to adopt this figure instead of 36,000,000 of the report of 1869-70, as the <em>Englishman</em> of 25th June, 1872, states that the census of Bengal, as far as the figures are made up, leads to an estimate of about 65,000,000.  Again, as in the North-West Provinces, I allow five-sixths of an acre of cultivated land per head, and take, therefore, 54,000,000 acres of cultivated land for a population of 65,000,000.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">With regard to produce, coarse rice is the chief produce of Bengal, and in taking it to represent the whole produce, I shall be near enough the mark.  For the produce of rice per acre, I take a table given in the report of the Indigo Commission (Parliamentary Return No. 72,1 of 1861, page 292), in which produce of paddy per beegah is given for a number of districts.  The rough average, without reference to the quantity of land in each district, comes to about nine maunds per beegah.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The maund I take is the Indian maund of 82 lbs.  The quantity of produce per beegah given in the table is evidently for rice in husk; for, though not so stated, this would be apparent by comparing the money values of these quantities given in the same table, with the prices for 1860 given in the table at page 291.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The beegah I find explained, at page lxi of the same return, at about one-third of an acre.  Thacker’s Bengal Directory for 1872, page 2, gives the following table for “Bengal square or land measure”:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>1 chittack = 45 square feet or 5 square yards. 16       ,,    =   1 cottah = 720 sqr. ft. or 80 sqr. yds. 20 cottah =   1 beegah = 14,400  ,,  or 1,600   ,,</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thus gives a little more than 3 beegahs to an acre.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Cowasjee Eduljee, the manager of the Port Canning rice mills and lands, thinks, that for an average of all lands, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-34" data-page="34" data-folio="14" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n33_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 14">[p. 14]</button> or say for standard land, seven maunds of paddy per beegah will be a very fair calculation. I take eight maunds. Mr. Cowasjee further says, as the out-turn of his mills, that paddy yields 55 per cent. of rice at the outside.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For the price of rice I take the season 1867-8. I take the rough average of the weekly prices given in the Calcutta <em>Gazette</em> for the months of January to March, 1868, as fairly representing the effect of the season of 1867-8. This average is taken by simply adding up the prices and dividing by the number of districts, and not on the correct principle of taking the quantities of the produce of each district into account (as in specimen table A I have given for the Central Provinces). The average, therefore, which I have adopted, must be much higher than the actual one, and will require some reasonable deduction. I shall deduct only 10 per cent. as some correction for this, and to make up for any error in the produce per acre. Besides, the prices given in the <em>Gazette</em> are retail prices, and are therefore higher than the prices all over the country; so my deduction of 10 per cent. will be but a very small correction for all the errors of my rough calculation. I cannot get the extent of cultivated land for each district. I give below the calculations. Since writing these notes, I have seen the late census report, which gives the population as 66,856,859, or say 67,000,000. The approximate area of cultivated land will be, say, five-sixths of 67,000,000 or 56,000,000 acres. The produce per acre, taken as 24 maunds paddy per acre, will give about 13 maunds of clean rice, or 1,066 lbs., say 1,100 lbs. The total produce of 56,000,000 acres will be 616,000,000 lbs., which, at 58 lbs. per rupee (as obtained by the rough average of the weekly prices of the three months of January, February, and March, 1868), will give Rs. 1,06,00,000, or £106,000,000. Deducting 10 per cent. will give £95,400,000, or say £96,000,000, for a population of 67,000,000. This will amply cover the higher price of some of the articles, such as silk, indigo, cost price of opium, tea, etc., or any double crops, etc. The percentage of these products is a small one; the total value for all these will be under 10 per cent. of the whole produce, while the average of price I have taken for rice as representing the whole produce of the Presidency will be found much above the actuals. On the whole, I cannot help thinking that the total value of all productions of the Bengal Presidency will be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-35" data-page="35" data-folio="15" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n34_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 15">[p. 15]</button> found much under, than above, my estimate. It is very desirable, however, to get a correct result, and the Statistical Committee or Agricultural Department should give it.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Madras.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take the administration report of 1868-9 as I have not been able to get an opportunity of studying that of 1867-8. Besides, as prices have not much altered, the later report is the better. I am obliged to ascertain the produce per acre from other sources: the report does not give the information. I take paddy to represent the produce of wet, and cumboo for dry land, as they form the bulk of the produce of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. H. Newill, the Director of Settlements for South Arcot, in his letter of 27th August, 1859 (Selections of the Madras Government, No. 14, of 1869, Appendix Y, from page 142), gives an elaborate table of produce per acre of the principal grains, as ascertained by a large number of experiments and general enquiry; and the result of his investigations gives, for the different classes of soils, the following produce, from which 5 per cent. is to be deducted for numerous ridges for regulating irrigation channels, exterior boundaries, etc.:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-009"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-009 · FOLIO 35</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-009_p0035.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-009_p0035.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>Produce of Wet Land per acre for “Good Crop” first grade Land—</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Description of Soils.</th><th scope="col">Value assigned for Good Crops per Acre H. C. (Bazar Huris Cullum).</th><th scope="col">Description of Soils.</th><th scope="col">Value assigned for Good Crops per Acre H. C. (Bazar Huris Cullum).</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">45</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">30</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">40</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">25</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">35</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">20</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">30</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">18</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">28</td><td>14 15</td><td class="num">15</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">6</td><td class="num">40</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">35</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">30</td><td>Average</td><td class="num">30</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">28</td><td></td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Deducting 5 per cent. for ridges, etc., 30—1½ = 28½ H. C.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For second grade land, deduct 15 per cent., which will give 24¼ H. C. For third grade deduct 20 per cent., which will give 22·8 H. C. For bad seasons Mr. Newill deducts 10 per cent. more, which I do not; so that the produce calculated by me is for “good crop,” or in “good season,” as in all other cases. Taking second grade as the bulk of the land, I take 24¼ H. C. as the average of all wet land.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-36"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="36" data-folio="16" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n35_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 16">[p. 16]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For dry land for cumboo (page 150), Mr. Newill gives the produce per acre as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-010"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-010 · FOLIO 36</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-010_p0036.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-010_p0036.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col">Descriptions of Soil.</th><th scope="col">H. C.</th><th scope="col">Descriptions of Soils.</th><th scope="col">H. C.</th><th scope="col">Descriptions of Soils.</th><th scope="col">H. C.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">21</td><td class="num">6</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">12</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">15</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">17</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">16</td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">14</td><td class="num">9</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">14</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">14</td><td class="num">15</td><td class="num">8</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td colspan="4"></td><td>Average . .</td><td>13 <sup>11</sup>/<sub>15</sub> say 14 H. C.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The next thing necessary is to ascertain the correct average price. I take the average price as given in the administration report (calculated on the wrong principle referred to by me before), bearing in mind that the correct average, as worked out according to specimen table A, would be very likely found lower. Again, taking the rough average of first and second-class paddy, the price comes to Rs. 180 per garce; and as second-class paddy must be the bulk of the produce, the correct average price in this respect also must be lower. In taking, therefore, Rs. 180 per garce, some reasonable allowance will have to be made. I shall make it only 10 per cent. for all kinds of excess. It is too much work for me to calculate as in table A.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wet land under cultivation (except South Canara and Malabar, where areas under cultivation are not given), is, for 1868 9, 2,957,748 acres at 24¼ H. C. produce per acre (and 133⅓ H. C. = 1 garce<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-005" href="#sec-04-fn-005" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-005" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>) will give 511,490 garces, which, at Rs. 180 per garce, will give Rs. 9,68,53,500—the total value of the produce of wet land.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dry cultivated land (except South Canara and Malabar) is 13,560,329 acres, and with produce at 14 H. C. per acre (and 133 H. C. = 1 garce), will give 1,427,403 garces. I take the rough average price as given in the table—Rs. 188 per garce—in the administration report of 1868-9. This will be an over-estimate, as quantities in each district are not taken into account. The total value will be—1,427,403 garces at Rs. 188 = Rs. 26,83,51,764. Total produce of wet and dry lands will be Rs. 36,52,05,264; adding 10 per cent. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-37" data-page="37" data-folio="17" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n36_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 17">[p. 17]</button> for South Canara and Malabar, the total for all the Madras Presidency will be a little above Rs. 400,000,000. From this is to be allowed 10 per cent. as a correction for errors of high averages, which will leave, say, £36,000,000 for a population of 26,539,052 (Parliamentary Return No. <sup>(C. 184)</sup><sub>1870</sub>), or say 26,500,000.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Bombay</span>.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The season 1867-8 was a favourable one (Bombay administration report, 1867-8, page 59); that for 1868-9 unfavourable (report for 1868-9, page 65). I take the former to ascertain the produce of a fair good season. I am sorry that the administration reports give no agricultural information. I therefore take the necessary particulars from other sources. The Revenue Commissioner’s reports for 1867-8 give the total area under cultivation for the Northern Division at 5,129,754 acres and 1,263,139 beegahs, in which are included for grass and fallow-land 611,198 acres and 226,708 beegahs. The actual cultivated land will, after deducting this, be 4,518,556 acres, and 1,036,431 beegahs = 609,842 acres, or total acres, 5,128,398. Out of this, bajri, jowari, rice, and cotton make up nearly two-thirds, or above 60 per cent., as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-011"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-011 · FOLIO 37</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-011_p0037.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-011_p0037.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Acres.</th><th scope="col">Beegahs.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Bajri</td><td class="num">985,427</td><td class="num">56,857</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Jowari</td><td class="num">676,377</td><td class="num">224,210</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice.</td><td class="num">616,802</td><td class="num">94,306</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cotton</td><td class="num">519,058</td><td class="num">319,572</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td class="num">2,797,664</td><td>694,945 = 408,791 acres,</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">or total acres 3,206,455.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Similarly for the Southern Division, out of the total acres, 13,985,892, jowari, bajri, rice, and cotton make up above 60 per cent. as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-012"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-012 · FOLIO 37</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-012_p0037.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-012_p0037.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Acres.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Jowari</td><td class="num">4,906,073</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bajri</td><td class="num">2,715,719</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice</td><td class="num">504,015</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cotton</td><td class="num">704,629</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td class="num">8,830,436</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take, therefore, these four articles to represent the produce of the whole Presidency, though this will give a higher estimate. Neither the administration nor the Revenue Commissioner’s reports give produce per acre or prices. I take these two items as follows. From selections of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-38" data-page="38" data-folio="18" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n37_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 18">[p. 18]</button> Bombay Government, Nos. 10 and 11 of 1853, I get the following estimate of produce:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-013"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-013 · FOLIO 38</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-013_p0038.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-013_p0038.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>Produce per Acre in Pounds.</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col" colspan="2">Selections.</th><th scope="col" rowspan="2">Districts Reported upon.</th><th scope="col">Bajri with Kuthole.</th><th scope="col">Jowari with Kuthole.</th><th scope="col">Sathi, or Coarse Rice.</th><th scope="col">Kupas, or uncleaned Cotton.</th><th scope="col" rowspan="2">Remarks.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col">No.</th><th scope="col">Page.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>X.</td><td class="num">15</td><td>Prant of Husore— Morassa &amp; Bayar Pergunnah in Ahmedabad Collectorate ..</td><td class="num">680</td><td>700 1,020 Jowari in fallow land.</td><td class="num">1,020</td><td></td><td rowspan="6">Cleaned Cotton as per experiments made under order of Mr. Saunders, Resident of Hyderabad, in Bassein district of Berar—average of 8 acres giving 31¾ lbs. of clean Cotton and 83½ lbs. of Seed. (<em>Agricultural Gazette of India</em> of 21st Aug. 1871, page 3.) This would give 82 lbs. for 305 lbs. of kupas.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td class="num">106</td><td>Duskroee Pergunnah— Greatest ..</td><td class="num">1,700</td><td class="num">1,500</td><td class="num">1,360</td><td class="num">410</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Least . .</td><td class="num">270</td><td class="num">210</td><td class="num">410</td><td class="num">200</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>XI.</td><td class="num">15</td><td>Dholka— Greatest ..</td><td class="num">1,700</td><td class="num">1,500</td><td class="num">1,360</td><td class="num">410</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Least . .</td><td class="num">270</td><td class="num">210</td><td class="num">410</td><td class="num">200</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Rough average . . .</td><td class="num">924</td><td class="num">856</td><td class="num">912</td><td class="num">305</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The above averages belong to a fertile part of the Northern Division, so that if I put down 900 lbs. for bajri, jowari, and rice per acre, and 80 lbs. of cotton for the whole of that Division, I shall be making a high estimate.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The next thing to settle is the prices. I take them from the <em>Government Gazette</em> weekly prices for the months of January to May, 1868, as fairly representing the effect of the average favourable season of 1867-8. These are retail prices of the chief markets of the respective districts, and it will be necessary to deduct 10 per cent. to make a fair average for the whole of the Division. For cotton I take the export prices from the Prices Current of the Bombay Chamber of Commerce for January to May, 1868. This gives an average of Rs. 181 per candy. The export prices I have taken represent more than the average value of the whole crop of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-39" data-page="39" data-folio="19" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n38_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 19">[p. 19]</button> Presidency, as the above average is for Fair Dhollera and Bhownuggur, which necessarily give a higher figure than the average of all the different varieties. Again, the bulk of the cotton is not “fair,” but “mid-fair”; so, to make a fair allowance for all these circumstances, I take the price for 1867-8 as Rs. 170 per candy of 784 lbs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>The Southern Division.</em>—As a whole, this Division is not as fertile as the Northern. I shall take, however, only 50 lbs. less for bajri, jowari, and rice; and for cotton I take 60 lbs. per acre—a high average for the whole of the Division; for Mr. J. B. Smith, M.P., in his paper of 1857 read before the Society of Arts, quotes Mr. Vary, the then late Superintendent of Government Cotton Experiments in Sattara and Sholapore, to the effect that “40 lbs. of clean cotton per acre is considered a fair crop.” For rice, I take Rutnagherry as exceptional in its produce. If I give 1,700 lbs. per acre for the whole district, it will be a high average.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-006" href="#sec-04-fn-006" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-006" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> I take the prices from the <em>Government Gazette</em> in the same way as for the Northern Division, and a similar reduction of 10 per cent. will have to be made. I give below a table worked out in the manner described above:—</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-40"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="40" data-folio="20" Rs. 17.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-020"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-020 · FOLIO 42</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-020_p0042.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-020_p0042.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>Total Cultivated Area.</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Acres.</th><th scope="col">Rs.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Northern Division .</td><td>5,128,221 at Rs. 28·51 =</td><td class="num">14,62,05,580</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Southern „ .</td><td>13,985,892 „ 17 =</td><td class="num">23,77,60,164</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total . . .</td><td></td><td class="num">Rs. 38,39,65,744</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This gives for the whole of the Bombay Presidency the total value as Rs. 38,39,65,744, or say £40,000,000 for a population of 11,000,000.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">About two or three months ago I came across an unexpected confirmation of my calculations. I was able to get from my friend, Mr. Nowrojee Furdoonjee, a few notes from Colonel Prescott’s reports on the settlement of Akleshwar Taluka—I suppose an average Gujerat taluka. Colonel Prescott has made the value of gross produce (excluding straw) about Rs. 24 per acre. Why, my estimate for the whole of the Northern Division is above Rs. 28 per acre.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Oudh.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The administration report does not give the agricultural tables, but they are given in the revenue report. Wheat <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-43" data-page="43" data-folio="23" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n42_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 23">[p. 23]</button> forms the most important produce in Oudh, as in the North-West Provinces. I take it to represent the whole produce. In the revenue report ending 30th September, 1868, the average produce per acre is given at 892 lbs.—say 900 lbs. Now, in Oudh, irrigated land is nearly within 10 per cent. of unirrigated land. I shall give the above produce per acre for both, as the table also gives this as the average of all land. The year 1867-8 was somewhat below an average good season, and the prices, therefore, higher than they would be for an average good season year. I take them, however, as they are. The average for wheat, first quality, is given at Rs. 1-9-7 per maund of 80 lbs., and for second quality Rs. 1-8-4—the average will be about Rs. 1-9. As a small correction for the prices being of an inferior season, the average being on the usual wrong principle, and the second quality being the largest quantity, I shall deduct only 10 per cent. The total cultivated area is 12,486 square miles, or 7,991,040 acres. The total produce, at 900 lbs. of wheat per acre, will be 7,191,936,000 lbs.; and the total value, at the rate of Rs. 1-9 per maund of 80 lbs., will be Rs. 14,04,67,500. This, less 10 per cent., will be Rs. 12,64,20,750, or say £13,000,000, for a population of 9,500,000.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-021"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-021 · FOLIO 43</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-021_p0043.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-021_p0043.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><span class="sc">Summary.</span></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Provinces.</th><th scope="col">Value of the Produce of Cultivated Land.</th><th scope="col">Population.</th><th scope="col">Produce per head.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">£</th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Rs.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Central Provinces</td><td class="num">16,000,000</td><td class="num">9,000,000</td><td class="num">18</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Punjab</td><td class="num">36,000,000</td><td class="num">17,500,000</td><td class="num">21</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>North-West Provinces</td><td class="num">40,000,000</td><td class="num">30,000,000</td><td class="num">14</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bengal</td><td class="num">96,000,000</td><td class="num">67,000,000</td><td class="num">15</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Madras</td><td class="num">36,000,000</td><td class="num">26,500,000</td><td class="num">14</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bombay</td><td class="num">40,000,000</td><td class="num">11,000,000</td><td class="num">36</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Oudh</td><td class="num">13,000,000</td><td class="num">9,500,000</td><td class="num">14</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total</td><td class="num">277,000,000</td><td class="num">170,500,000</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Such is the produce of India for a good season year, in which any second crops will be fully included. I have not taken the produce of grazing-land, or straw, or kurby, though the cattle required for cultivation and stock need not only all <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-44" data-page="44" data-folio="24" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n43_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 24">[p. 24]</button> these grazing-lands, but also a portion of the produce of the cultivated land, such as some grains, fodder, and other produce. From the above total of £277,000,000 it is necessary to deduct for seed for next year, say, only 6 per cent., that is, allowing sixteen-fold for produce of the land. The balance will be about £260,000,000 as the produce of cultivation, during a good season, for human use and consumption for a year. If the Government of India would calculate this production correctly, it would find the total a good deal under the above figures.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Other Items of India’s Wealth.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have next to add for annual produce of stock for consumption, annual value of manufacturing industry, net opium revenue, cost of production of salt, coals, and mines, and profits of foreign commerce.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Salt, opium, coal, and profits of commerce will be about £17,000,000. For annual price of manufacturing industry or stock, I have not come across full particulars. The manufacturing industry in the Punjab—where there are some valuable industries, such as shawls, silks, etc., to the total estimated value of the “annual out-turns of all works”—is put down as about £3,774,000. From this we deduct the value of the raw produce; and if I allow this value to be doubled by all the manufactures, I shall be making a good allowance. Say, then, that the value of the industry is about £2,000,000, including the price of wool; the manufactures of other parts of India are not quite as valuable. Therefore, for the population of all British India, which is about ten times that of the Punjab, if I take £15,000,000 for the value of manufacturing industry, I shall not be far from the mark. The total for Central Provinces for 1870-1 for all manufactures is about £1,850,000. There are no very valuable industries; allow, therefore, £850,000 for the value of the industry for a population of 9,000,000. In this proportion, the total value for India will be about, say, £17,000,000. For the annual produce of stock, and fish for human consumption, as milk or meat, I can hardly get sufficient data to work upon. I hope Government will give the particulars more fully, so that the annual production of stock for consumption, either as milk or meat, may be known. I set it down as £15,000,000 as a guess only. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-45" data-page="45" data-folio="25" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n44_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 25">[p. 25]</button> All this will make up a total of about £307,000,000. I add for any contingencies another £30,000,000, making at the utmost £340,000,000 for a population of 170,000,000, or 40s. a head for an average <em>good season.</em> I have no doubt that, if the Statistical Department worked out the whole correctly and fully, they would find the total less. Again, when further allowance is made for bad seasons, I cannot help thinking that the result will be nearer 30s. than 40s. a head. One thing is evident—that I am not guilty of any under-estimate of produce.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Income per Head.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Adding this additional £63,000,000 in proportion of population, that is to say 7s. 5d. per head, the total production per head of each province will be as follows:—Central Provinces, 43s. 5d.; Punjab, 49s. 5d.; N. W. Provinces, 35s. 5d.; Bengal, 37s. 5d.; Madras, 35s. 5d.; Bombay, 79s. 5d.; Oudh, 35s. 5d.—Average, 40s.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Necessary Consumption.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I now consider what is necessary for the bare wants of a human being, to keep him in ordinary good health and decency.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have calculated production chiefly for the year 1867-8. I shall take the same year for ascertaining the necessary consumption.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Surgeon S. B. Partridge, Government Medical Inspector of Emigrants, in a statement dated Calcutta, 26th March, 1870,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-011" href="#sec-04-fn-011" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-011" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> proposes the following as a scale of diet to supply the necessary ingredients of nourishment for the emigrant coolies during their voyage, living in a state of quietude:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-022"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-022 · FOLIO 45</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-022_p0045.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-022_p0045.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col" colspan="2">Rice Diet for One Man.</th><th scope="col" colspan="2">For Flour Diet</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">ozs.</th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">ozs.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice</td><td class="num">20·0</td><td>Flour</td><td class="num">16·0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Dhal</td><td class="num">6·0</td><td>Dhal.</td><td class="num">4·0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Preserved Mutton</td><td class="num">2·5</td><td>Preserved Mutton</td><td class="num">2·5</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Vegetables</td><td class="num">4·27</td><td>Vegetables</td><td class="num">4·27</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ghee</td><td class="num">1·0</td><td>Ghee</td><td class="num">1·5</td></tr><tr Again, for confirmed opium-eaters or <em>ganja</em>-smokers, the surgeon superintendent is to see a proper quantity supplied. Surgeon Partridge’s scale is absolutely necessary to supply the necessary ingredients of nitrogen and carbon; not the slightest luxury—no sugar or tea, or any little enjoyment of life, but simple animal subsistence of coolies living in a state of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-47" data-page="47" data-folio="27" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n46_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 27">[p. 27]</button> quietude. I have worked out below the cost of living according to Surgeon Partridge’s scale for the year 1867-8 at Ahmedabad prices. The scale in the Bengal administration report provides curry-stuff and narcotics in addition, which I have not calculated in this table, though it can hardly be said that they are not necessaries to those poor people.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-024"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-024 · FOLIO 47</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a further on, to calculate the cost per head of family, the result will be—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-029"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-029 · FOLIO bad seasons.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The report of the Bombay Price Commission gives the following particulars of the wants of the lowest servants of Government (pages 85. class="num">1</td><td class="num">11</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Grand Total.</td><td>Rs.</td><td class="num">8</td><td>6 per month.</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For Poona the above scale is calculated to cost Rs. 6-11 per month, or Rs. 80-4 per annum, at the high prices of 1863, while my estimate, according to Surgeon Partridge’s scale for 1867-8, is Rs. 5-2-10 per month, or Rs. 62-2 per annum— nearly 24 per cent. less, as prices have gone lower. For clothing, the estimate for 1863 is Rs. 1-11 per month, or Rs. 20-8 per annum, while Mr. Shahabudin’s estimate is only Rs. 8-2 in 1868. Even allowing for fall in price Mr. Shahabudin’s estimate is lower, and calculated on a very low scale for an agricultural labourer in the poorest districts, while that of 1863 is for the lowest class of Government servants. Upon the whole, therefore, the estimate given for 1867-8, as for the bare necessaries of a common agricultural labourer, is evidently under the mark.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lately I found the following in the “Statement of the Moral and Material Progress of India” for 1871-2:—“The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-50" data-page="50" data-folio="30" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n49_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 30">[p. 30]</button> best account of the Bombay peasantry is still probably that by Mr. Coats, written fifty years ago. The clothes of a man then cost about 12s. and the furniture of his house about £2.”—(Parliamentary Return No. 172 of 28th April, 1873.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have not been able to work out the details of cost of living in other parts of India. For the present I give the following approximate comparison for 1867-8:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-031"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-031 · FOLIO 50</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-031_p0050.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a colspan="3">Cost of Clothing.</th><th scope="col" colspan="3">Total.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col">Rs.</th><th scope="col">a.</th><th scope="col">p.</th><th scope="col">Rs.</th><th scope="col">a.</th><th scope="col">p.</th><th scope="col">Rs.</th><th scope="col">a.</th><th scope="col">p.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Central Provinces</td><td class="num">25</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">31</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Punjab</td><td class="num">23</td><td class="num">6</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">27</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>North-West Provinces</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">21</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bengal<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-017" href="#sec-04-fn-017" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-017" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></td><td class="num">28</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">31</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Madras</td><td class="num"><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-018" href="#sec-04-fn-018" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-018" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>49</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">15</td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">53</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bombay</td><td class="num">41</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">47</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Oudh</td><td colspan="3">.........</td><td colspan="3">.........</td><td colspan="3">.........</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">PROPORTION OF CHILDREN TO ADULTS.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now, the Bengal Census Report of 1872, page 109 of the appendix, gives the percentage of population according to age as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-032"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-032 · FOLIO 50</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-032_p0050.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-032_p0050.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col" colspan="2">Males.</th><th scope="col" colspan="2">Females.</th><th scope="col" rowspan="2"></th></tr><tr><th scope="col">Not exceeding 12 Years.</th><th scope="col">Above 12 Years.</th><th scope="col">Not exceeding 12 Years.</th><th scope="col">Above 12 Years.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">18·8</td><td class="num">31·3</td><td class="num">15·7</td><td class="num">34·2</td><td>The Census of the N.W. Provinces gives nearly the same result. Above 12 years, adults, 64·4 per cent.; under 12, 35·6 per cent. (See Administration Report for 1871—72, page 55; Census Report, vol. 1, page 31.)</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-51"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="51" data-folio="31" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n50_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 31">[p. 31]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The total adults, that is, above 12 years, are 65·5 per cent., and infants or children under 12 years, 34·5 per cent., which gives the proportion of two adults to each child, or one child to every three persons.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Production Compared with Cost of Living.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">From taking the cost of adults per head to be <em>a</em>, and cost of the mass per head to be <em>x</em>, and supposing that, out of 34 per cent. of children under 12, only 17 per cent. cost anything, say one-half of the adult (though the Bengal provision is half for children from two to ten years), while the other 17 cost nothing at all, the problem will be—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">66<em>a</em> + 17 <em><sup>a</sup></em>/<sub>2</sub> + 17 × 0 = 100<em>x x</em> = <sup>74½<em>a</em></sup>/<sub>100</sub>, or say <sup>75<em>a</em></sup>/<sub>100</sub> or ¾<em>a</em>; <em>i.e.</em>, the cost outside jail, or for the whole mass per head, will be about three-fourths of inside the jail, allowing the jail for adults only. Thus, taking the cost of three persons in the jail, or of three adults to four persons outside, or of the mass, it comes to this:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-033"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-033 · FOLIO 51</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tables/tbl-033_p0051.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/poverty-and-un-british-rule/datasets/tidy/tbl-033_p0051.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col">Production per Head.</th><th scope="col">Three-fourths of Jail Cost of Living, or Cost per head outside Jail.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Central Provinces Rs. 21¾ or say Rs. 22</td><td class="num">Rs. 23</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Punjab „ 24¾ „ „ 25</td><td>„ 20</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>North-West Provinces „ 17¾ „ „ 18</td><td>„ 16</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Madras „ 17¾ „ „ 18</td><td>„ 41</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bengal „ 18¾ „ „ 19</td><td>„ 23-12</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bombay „ 39¾ „ „ 40</td><td>„ 35</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Oudh „ 17¾ „ „ 18</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It will be seen, from a comparison of the above figures, that, even for such food and clothing as a criminal obtains, there is hardly enough of production even in a good season, leaving alone all little luxuries, all social and religious wants, all expenses of occasions of joy and sorrow, and any provision for bad season. It must, moreover, be borne in mind that every poor labourer does not get the full share of the average production. The high and middle classes get a much larger share, the poor classes much less, while the lowest cost of living is generally above the average share.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Such appears to be the condition of the masses of India. They do not get enough to provide the bare necessaries of life.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-52"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="52" data-folio="32" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n51_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 32">[p. 32]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the subject of necessary consumption, I shall be very glad if some members of this Association, or others who possess or can ascertain the necessary information, will supply it, as I have not been able to make such minute and extended enquiries myself as I could wish.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Deficit of Imports Compared with the Exports of India.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The total imports and exports of the United Kingdom for the years 1858 to 1870 are—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-034"><div
